Let us now zoom in and enter the small-scale realm. When dealing with continuoustime representations, we use the complex pseudo-baseband channel response. We
also recall from Section 2.2.3 that, to discretize this representation, lowpass
filtering is in general required before sampling, although in those cases when the
discretization returns a singletap response and the effect is only multiplicative, the
complex gain of the pseudo-baseband channel can be borrowed directly.

Due to the various NLOS effects, what the receiver observes is a superposition of a
number of replicas of the transmitted signal; the overall channel response is a sum of all
these various contributions.
Each path can be described using the attenuation A and the delay τ , under the form
q

modified by the local reflection/diffraction/scattering, and thus the complex gains
{A } may not exhibit major differences. However, because at carrier frequencies of

In class
If we're in the narrowband case (we have a narrow band signal), this means
that the symbol duration is much larger than the inverse of the bandwidth.
This means that we have a large symbol, then a delay, the symbols are more or
less aligned:

We can use the same coefficient but they might have a different phase (blue).
We thus assume we have just one delta and that's where the second summation
(the one on the right) comes from.
In this case we have a one tap channel with a gain of √G ⋅ h. We know h is
random but must be either modulus one or variance 1.
This is modeled due to the Central Limit Theorem like a complex Gaussian with
. We have that such a random variable is irrotational. The phase varies
randomly between 0 and 2π.

The signal reaching the receiver will thus be

08.3_pp_131_208_Channel_modeling, page 14
Thus, upon a sinusoid the channel does have only a multiplicative effect, i.e., a
complex gain. Furthermore, the preceding consideration on the path phases suggests
modeling this gain as a sum of IID random variables; if the number of paths is large
enough and some mild conditions are satisfied [221], application of the central limit
theorem leads to a complex Gaussian distribution. The result of the sum in (3.21)
changes rapidly with the position of both transmitter and receiver: a mere
displacement on the order of a wavelength alters the path lengths, and thus the
delays thereon, by an amount that shifts the path phases drastically, causing major
swings in the sum. Put differently, the paths can add constructively or destructively
depending on their relative phases, which are completely modified by small changes
in position. The same effect transpires if, instead of a change in position, there is a
change in frequency that shifts the phases substantially.

08.3_pp_131_208_Channel_modeling, page 14
The picture that emerges is thus as follows: the large-scale channel features
(pathloss and shadow fading) determine the average channel conditions within the
local neighborhood while the small-scale fading determines, subject to that localaverage, the instantaneous channel conditions at each position and frequency. This
invites decoupling the complex gain as in (3.21), namely √G ⋅ h where √G determines
the local-average received power, P , as per (3.5), whereas the small-scale fading h
r

is normalized to have unit power. This largely achieves the desired separation
between large-scale and small-scale features, yet a mild coupling remains in that
certain other parameters of the local small-scale fading, e.g., the Rice factor, may
depend on (or be correlated with) the large-scale gain. Ideally, the value of the
large-scale features should be taken into account when setting those smallscale
parameters, or else there is a danger that the hugely convenient decoupling between
large- and small-scale gains entails a loss of relevant information.

The small-scale fading is locally modeled as a stationary random process, in principle
zero-mean and complex Gaussian, normalized to have unit variance. At a given position
and frequency, then, it is h ∼ N (0, 1) with uniformly distributed phase and with a
C

magnitude that abides by the Rayleigh distribution (see Appendix C.1.9), hence the
popular designation of small-scale fading as simply Rayleigh fading. Precisely,

Generalization to Rice
fading

If a multipath term dominates over the rest, e.g., because it propagates through LOS
and it is subject to a distinct pathloss, then the receiver can lock onto that signal
component and render it unfaded. It follows that h ∼ N (μ , σ ) with mean μ
variance σ = 1 − |μ | . For this so-called Rice fading, we have
2

where K = |μ | /σ is the Rice factor while I (⋅) is the zero-order modified Bessel function
2

0

of the first kind.
In class
The K factor is the ratio between the mean signal strength and the variance of a
random component.

For K = 0, Rice fading reverts to Rayleigh fading; for K → ∞, the fading vanishes as the
value of h becomes deterministic. Thus, the Rice distribution bridges these extremes and
allows, through K, to regulate the severity of the fading. Analytically speaking,
however, the PDF in (3.24) is not particularly friendly because of the presence of I (⋅).

Digression on the importance of channel
modeling+standardization institutions
We were talking about the channel models; a few words before the actual lecture:
tomorrow we'll have a regular lecture.
About this chapter: in general there's not a well-shaped channel modeling theory; if we
feel we're confused, it's normal at the first approach. At the beginning we said we're
trying to make a mathematical model for a really complicated environment: to make a
deterministic model for such a situation would be too difficult by far.
We resorted to models which should mimic reality. Unfortunately, we don't have an
agreed model; for the noise, we know that we have WGN. Here we have a confusing
situation: what we want to design depends on our purpose; we have models which are
useful in certain situations, other models which are useful in certain others.
Specifically, we're looking for the limits of our systems; when we know what the
ultimate limit is, we know how far we are from that ultimate limit.
If we are at 3dB from the ultimate performance, there's room for improvement: our
effort can pay off. If we're 0.01dB from the limit, our work is done from an engineering
perspective; even if we improve, we're really close to the theoretical limit.
When we approach this chapter, we know we have many different models; some are
useful (e.g.: a model has gaussian variables as an input of the system). In specific
cases we don't have gaussian values, though.

Important point is that we have very different models depending on what we're doing.
In this chapter we also find practical stuff btw. When we talk about LTE and 5G, who
defines what those are?
Standardization is really complicated; internet standards is handled by the IETF (Internet
Engineering Task Force): it establishes protocols. Other things standardized are how the
wireless network is done and how different networks can communicate; what most
interests us is the communication interface: there must be a precise standard to allow
communication between different towers etc etc; this is called radio interface.
There must be a document saying how we transmit every single bit; we want to have a
competitive market, in which multiple brands can improve on the communication.
In the past, mobile phones were chosen depending on reception capabilities; now we
have a field so high that it's very easy to receive signal.
We need to have specifications about sending bits from the central tower to a mobile
device and vice versa.
There is not a specification on how the mobile phone should be made, but rather only on
how the phone should communicate.
If we know how bits are sent downlink, we might think to use ZF or even better,
LMMSE; it must be specified how bits are sending data to the base stations.
We can make base stations however we want: we're the ones deciding the compromise
between cost and performance.

For cellular standards we have 3GPP, which is third generation ?? program. How can we
participate in specification of 3GPP? We can't do that easily: it's made by several
regional
standard
organizations;
here
in
Europe
we
have
ETSI
(European
Telecommunications Standard Institute). In Japan we have ARIB, ....
Who is member of those partners can send standards to 3GPP; we can be members if we
pay a fee, which depends on turnover of company, ...; in the 3GPP is dealing with radio
access networks. How do they decide if we use OFDM or whatever?
3: why? 1: analog, 2: GSM, 3: after GSM, 4: LTE, 5G, 6G, ...
2G was specified by ETSI and adopted by other countries. For 3G they wanted to have
global roaming; that's how 3GPP was formed.
It was decided that 3G would use WCDMA (Wideband CDMA). Let's suppose we have
different technologies.
Today we have OTFS, which is a way to transmit bits; how do we decide in a group
which of the various options we have is better? What happens is that we do theoretical
simulations; in all of this exercise there is a critical point: which channel model do we
use? Depending on the model, it might happen that a technology is better than another;
an initial debate was in fact on which channel model should have been used. The first
thing we need to do when developing standards is agreeing on the channel model.
In section 3.8 we'll find MIMO in standards.
Different groups adapt different models; there are researches being made on identifying
good and representative channel models.
Professor advises us to go through the chapter and then go back and redo it, since the
picture will be much clearer. We cannot forget reality; at the same time, we're doing
something inspired by reality.

Space selectivity
In class
We have a factor modulating the straight path and the other paths; the average of
the sum is given by the straight, while the average of the power is given by
considering all.

Space selectivity; we need somehow to have this. the two locations could be the

locations of the two antennas; let's say we have a wave which is hitting the two
antennas:

We know that if we go far the curvature is almost null.

There is a difference of length at various points: this will also mean we have a
difference in phase.
This difference matters a lot; this means that selectivity stays not in frequency, but
in space.
In the previous drawing we might have a phase perfect and a non-in-phase signal:
we can't just sum them, otherwise we'd have a problem.
We might have scattering clusters, if we don't do anything.
There are many resons to have space selectivity. We also have a PAS, but we'll see
it next week.
We know the spectrum of the signal tells us how the power is distributed on the
various frequencies.
If we talk about Power Angle, we talk about distribution depending on the angulation
of transmission. This is what PAS means.
In the previous drawing we were in a 2-d setting; we live in a 3-d setting; we
should consider not only the azimuth but also the elevation.
Generally we don't have influence in the elevation, while we have a lot of influence if
we change the azimuth.

PAS
What's the PAS?
From the book:
The phase shifts between different locations we referred to in equations such as (3.21),
are governed, at least in the local neighborhood, by the angle between the propagation
paths and the segment connecting those locations, as can be seen in the figure (which
shows the segment connecting two locations with a propagation path at an angle θ):

The change of path length between the two is simple given by Δ
the segment on the path direction.

, aka by projecting

D cos(θ)

Power Angle Spectrum (PAS)
Since the projection depends on θ, it is crucial to have a function which establishes
the angular distribution of paths; this function is the PAS (Power Angle Spectrum),
which expresses the received signal power as a function of angle when acting as a
receiver; instead, when we're acting as a transmitter, instead, the PA expresses
the transmitted signal power that reaches the receiver at the other end of the link as
a function of the angle of departure.

08.3_pp_131_208_Channel_modeling, page 16
The PAS is a continuous function of angle, normalized so it can be interpreted as a
PDF, which is a definition consistent with the unit-power normalization of the smallscale fading.

The standard deviation of the PAS, viewed as a distribution, gives the root-mean-square
(RMS) angle spread.
08.3_pp_131_208_Channel_modeling, page 16
Although, in principle, the definition of the PAS as a continuous function of angle
models a diffuse propagation scenario, it does not preclude modeling situations where

power is received only from a finite number of discrete angles; these situations can
be accommodated by constructing the PAS on the basis of discrete delta functions.
Formally, the PAS is a function of both azimuth and elevation. However, empirical
measurements have indicated that in most outdoor wireless systems the dependence
on elevation plays only a secondary role [224]. Historically then, the focus has been
on characterizing the marginal PAS on the azimuth plane—in fact, PAS can also be
taken to stand for power azimuth spectrum—yet the elevation dimension should not
be carelessly dismissed in other types of deployment, e.g., indoors or in vertical
urban picocells. We return to this aspect at the end of the chapter. Several functions
have been proposed and applied to model the marginal PAS in azimuth, which in this
text is denoted by P(⋅).
In class:
Last lesson, we were talking about space selectivity. We talk about it because we may
have 2 (or more) antennas: a part of the signal might be traveling a larger distance; in
our case, it is Δ cos(θ).
D

Normally, this is not so big that the strength of signal goes down, but the phase may
change. If we apply a phase shift on location 1, it's like as if we made the signal start
late. We can steer the wave with different angles by changing the phase of a certain
amount. This is the intuition behind what happens when a certain wave is hitting the two
antennas. If we have multiple antennas, we just apply the same principle.
We can thus make the antenna sensitive to a certain direction: even if we have an
omnidirectional antenna, the Power Angle Spectrum is modeling the fact that if we have
an antenna, we might have waves coming from multiple directions

What do we do in such a situation? The PAS is a probability distribution which tells where
it's most likely that the signal is arriving

We don't need to confuse the word spectrum being used here: we're not talking about
frequencies!

This allows us to understand where we can capture more energy. The PSA tells where we
have more power depending on the angle.

Antennas are generally 30m high and have a tilting on their top of a few degrees:

we need to decide the tilting depending on the application we want to have.
If we have a motor wave we have a central base station and then smaller others. If a
network sees we're jumping, it will assign us to an umbrella base station: the
probability that the calls drop is lower.

Clarke-Jakes PAS
We have various examples of PAS; Clarke-Jakes' is a uniform one:

1
P(θ) =

08.3_pp_131_208_Channel_modeling, page 16
It fits surprisingly well the behavior observed at mobile users, capturing the essence
of multipath propagation in a manner that is friendly to analysis. Such friendliness,
however, drops markedly as soon as the angular range is reduced below 2π and more
convenient alternatives exist if directional preference (particularly important in
transceivers located above the clutter, e.g., elevated base stations) is to be
introduced.

The Clarke-Jakes PAS is widely used. There are other spectrums related to this one, but

Simplified models
Why do we need simplified models?
08.3_pp_131_208_Channel_modeling, page 27
As mentioned at the outset of the chapter, the art of channel modeling must seek a
compromise between realism and tractability, distilling what is essential for each
purpose. When it comes to fading dynamics, it is sometimes desirable to have
models that capture the essence of time selectivity but are otherwise stripped down
to the bones. We next introduce two such models, formulated directly for the
discrete-time complex channel

Gauss-Markov model
A first possibility is to resort to a first-order Gauss–Markov process, or first-order
autoregressive process, whereby the fading at symbol n satisfies
h[n] = √ 1 − ϵh[n − 1] + √ϵw[n] ,

(3.61)

where {w[n]} is a sequence of IID random variables with w ∼ N (0, 1).
C

In turn, ϵ is the one-step prediction error, i.e. the variance of the error to which h[n]
can be predicted from a noiseless observation of h[n − 1], something that in a first-order
Markov process is as informative as a noiseless observation of the entire past. By
definition, a Gauss–Markov process is regular and its degree of decorrelation after ℓ
symbols is determined by ϵ via
∗

R h [ℓ] = E[h[n]h [n + ℓ]]
= (1 − ϵ)

ℓ/2

(3.62)

.

(3.63)

What people sometimes do is to say the next channel is the previous channel with a
factor √1 − ϵ and since we want to make it random, we add a bit of noise, w[n].
This is a random path: the channel is changing but not too much, because in the
ingredients we have a predictable part and also some noise.
This is called Gauss-Markov model. The correlation here is
distance between the sampling of the channel.
If we have ϵ → 0, the channel is completely stable.

(1 − ϵ)

A second possibility, illustrated in Fig. 3.12, is to resort to a block-fading structure,
whereby the fading remains constant over blocks of duration T while changing values
across blocks. It is worth noting that this model is not stationary but rather
c

cyclostationary with period T . The fading values at consecutive blocks may be
correlated, but are most often assumed IID.
c

only to allow us to apply the results which we saw for single carrier cases) we have per

However, care must be exercised with both (3.66) and (3.67) because they hold only if

They are most likely referring to the coherence bandwidth concept.
The block-fading model can be broadened to allow for continuous variability within
each block, with transitions to independent values across blocks. This more general
blockfading model can sometimes serve as a convenient bridge with a true continuous
fading model [245, 246].

We have jumps in time: we know that the signal has infinite bandwidth.

This is called block fading model: at every symbol it looks like we have fading, a jump.
We know this does not actually happen, in fact this is a model.

Now we'll talk about the coherence bandwidth B ; in order to do so, we define R which
looks kinda like a correlation.
c

08.3_pp_131_208_Channel_modeling, page 28
In our characterization of space and time selectivity, we have focused on the fading
experienced by a sinusoid (both for what concerns the influence of space correlation
and, although implicitly, the influence of time correlation) at frequency f . The
derivations hold approximately for frequencies close enough to f , and thus the fading
c

c

goes (roughly) unchanged over a certain frequency span. If the entire signal fits
within a swath of frequencies that experience (roughly) the same fading, the fading
is frequency-flat; the channel is then multiplicative, i.e., it merely applies a
complex gain. The signal can also be said to be narrowband, although such
narrowness must be understood only in reference to the fading.

08.3_pp_131_208_Channel_modeling, page 28
If the signal bandwidth is large enough, however, it is bound to exceed the range of
frequencies experiencing (roughly) the same fading. At a given location then, in
static conditions, the noiseless received signal is y(f ) = √Gh(f )x(f ) where h(f ) is no
longer constant over the bandwidth B. The signal undergoes frequency-selective
fading, and it can be said to be fading-wise wideband; the channel is not merely
multiplicative, but rather it effects a nontrivial convolution that modifies the shape of
the symbol pulses. The degree of selectivity over a frequency range Δ is quantified
by
f

∗

R h (Δ f ) = E[h(f )h (f + Δ f )]

whose chief measure is the coherence bandwidth B , the natural counterpart to the
coherence time and the coherence distance. The fading is frequency-flat if B ≪ B .
c

c

We ask ourselves the question: if we take a frequency and another frequency separated
of Δ from the first one, which is the difference in terms of the channel?
We can see that the function R does not depend on the frequency (in fact, it's
f

h

expressed as R (Δ ) and not as R (Δ , f )); this is not always true: it's like when we
show that the correlation of the stationary process only depends on the difference over
h

f

h

f

time.
Depending on what the shape of h(f ) is, we can define what the coherence bandwidth is.
Possible definition of Coherence Bandwidth B

c

The coherence bandwidth is sloppy as a concept; just to give an example of
definition:

we might decide it's 3dB from the top.

The coherence bandwidth is that bandwidth in which the channel stays almost constant;
this is extremely important when we do OFDM, since that tells us where we can position
a pilot without having a frequency-dependent distortion.

We can put the pilots spaced of not more than the coherence bandwidth; if we do more,
the channel in the middle can change.
This is definitely important; citing the slides: "Coherence bandwidth B determines the
bandwidths where the channel should be considered frequency selective".
c

This means that somehow in the previous example we have 2 different channels which
are frequency selective:

Normally, the time-invariant impulse response corresponding to h(f ) is h(τ ) = hδ(τ ), with h
being a complex gain at a given position. Of course, if we were to introduce motion, it'd
become time-dependent, giving h(t, τ ) = h(t)δ(τ ).

If we consider a motionless channel for which the minimum and maximum delays are
τ
,τ
, we'd then have that if τ
− τ
≪ T (with T being the symbol period), then
min

max

max

min

we'd have that the channel can still be modeled as h(τ ) = hδ(τ ) with little error.
If instead the condition is not satisfied, there is collision between the symbol n from a
certain path and the symbols ≠ n from other paths, causing not only fading but also ISI.
08.3_pp_131_208_Channel_modeling, page 29
To be sure, any form of fading is the result of self-interference among multipath
signal replicas of each symbol; the difference between frequency-flat and frequencyselective fading is that, in the former, every symbol essentially interferes
exclusively with replicas of itself, whereas in the latter it experiences significant
interference with adjacent symbols as well. The same exact channel can exhibit
either frequency-flat or frequency-selective fading depending on T or, equivalently,
depending on the bandwidth through which it is observed.

08.3_pp_131_208_Channel_modeling, page 29
Ultimately, the delay-dispersive nature of fading channels, and thus the frequency
selectivity, is rooted in the fact that the speed of light is finite. To appreciate this,
the reader is referred to Fig. 3.14, which depicts a transmit sequence of symbols
traveling over two distinct propagation paths. The finite value of c bestows upon the
symbol separation a finite length in space and opens the door to distinct symbols
colliding at the receiver if the difference in length between the paths is sufficiently
large. In contrast, if c were infinite, then the symbol sequences received over the

multiple paths would always align.
The foregoing interpretation can be further stretched to motivate the use of OFDM:
since ISI arises because of the finite length of the symbols, the antidote to ISI is to
make the symbols longer.

Fig 3.14

and coherence
bandwidth

frequencyflatness/selectivity

Power delay profile
and its relation with
the frequency
correlation

Delay spread in
continuous and
discrete cases

We can write down the power delay profile (PDP), which quantifies the energy
distributed as a function of delay in the time domain; it is normalized so that it can be
interpreted as a PDF (describing how likely we are to have a certain amount of power at

is still subject to fading.

is grounded in the reasonable premise that the propagation mechanisms giving
rise to paths having resolvable delays are essentially independent. Together with
the assumption of local stationarity, the uncorrelated scattering condition
underpins the traditional modeling of small-scale fading conforming the widesense stationary uncorrelated scattering (WSSUS) framework introduced by Bello
in 1963 [250]

In-class blabbering
When people are evaluating systems, they first of all establish the channel. The table
shows an example for the ITU; 3GPP sends its standards to ITU; rn ITU is working on
a standard called IMT-2030. Other organizations as well might be sending standards
for evaluation at ITU, which have other channel models which might as well be
different from the ones of 3GPP (3GPP might send 6G standard to ITU for evaluation).
5G has various techs, one of which is DECT-NR (NR: New Radio), which has been
recognized by ITU as a 5G standard.
We don't know which channel models reality the best; when we select a certain
technology, we want the tech to work well in both (or all) the channels we may be
considering!
When it comes to models, there's a lot of uncertainty.
The power density profile is different; one goes down, the other goes up and down.

About antenna arrays
Ref: 08.3_pp_131_208_Channel_modeling, page 40

Introduction (assumptions, array steering vector, array
manifold)
There's something we should pay attention to, even though there's also something we
already know.

We have a source and 2 receive points. In the figure below here it looks like (just like in
reality) the wave is a sphere; the curvature of the sphere becomes small if we're far
from where the wave is emitted.
The book is saying that we're allowing ourselves some simplifications:
Narrowband (or, in the book's terms, frequency flatness)
Far field (we can approximate the wave as if it was a plain)

Between receivers we have a common gain G, but a different phase. A small distance
changes the delay and attenuation by only a small amount, but it really changes the
phase, depending on f . If λ ∼ 1mm, moving 1mm causes a shift of phase which is
c

c

pretty intense.
We're now going to talk about arrays of antennas.
We assume that antennas are placed in an array, like in a line. Another practical case
might be that they're put in a circle (we have a base station and then various
antennas).

If we have narrowband signals, we can reduce our study to that of complex
exponentials: paths have common delay and attenuation, but different phase (just like
we already saw previously).
We then assume planar waves.

This picture is a bit confusing; if we take a reference and we use θ or −θ, it's the
same for what concerns cos(θ), as it is an even function.
What we care about are not absolute phases, but rather about the phase differences
between the two antennas.
For the situation in the figure above, the delay difference between the planar wavefronts
at each antenna will be equal to d cos(θ)/c and under frequency flatness (or using
narrowband signals) that will result in a phase shift of 2πd cos(θ)/λ (as we know from
previous results).
c

Such a delay will ultimately give

The narrowband assumption comes into play from the fact that we use only one λ: this
happens if we have just one exponential. If we have a narrowband signal, we'll
approximate that as if we had just one λ.
This is the case for 2G; if we take 900MHz+100KHz, we'll find that the difference in λ
will be pretty small, it won't change by a lot.
If we consider more things, the model will be treated randomly, since we have no
precise things (the antenna is not a point, ...).

a planar wave anymore. In reality, half of the antennas will not see the wave coming.
Some of them will have their aperture pointed towards the wave; this will be much more
difficult to be treated analytically.

08.3_pp_131_208_Channel_modeling, page 44
Letting w contain the weight coefficients, with w having unit-magnitude entries in

In LOS propagation, beamforming has the literal significance of geometrically shaping a
beam in θ.
We have the vector a(θ); sometimes we want to do beam forming: for each antenna, we
put a certain weight, which will cumulate to the vector w. A block scheme for this can
be the following (on the left we'll get A(θ))

If we do this, we can combine two effects:
Effect of the fact that the wave is coming from a certain direction; then we point our
wave in different directions by changing w, which is a weight for the phase. We
might put every w at 1 if we want to weight all the same.
If we're transmitting, we can alter the transmission so to have preferential
directions and such.
This is called analog beamforming. There are many papers on how to do this both in
analog and digital domain.
The whole formulation assumes that the antenna is isotopic; if it isn't, we need to
modify the array manifold vector to include pattern gains. So behind what we have,
there also is the isotropic antenna assumption, which can be reflected by changing the
array manifold.
Assuming the antenna isotropic is definitely an idealization.

IID Rayleigh-faded model

Rayleigh results from the rich scattering assumption: as we said before, this would not
work if we only were to have the LOS.
For the Wishart distribution, we should look at the appendix; it can be one or another
matrix depending on which value is bigger.

We then end up with the IID Rice-Faded model; this is also used for the SISO: we have a
part which is LOS, deterministic; then we have another matrix which is random. K is the
rice factor.
A generalization of the IID Rayleigh-faded model is the IID Rice-faded model

People tend to say, "I'd like to have a strong LOS and no scattering"; what happens is
that if we have a strong LOS, we won't get a full rank matrix. By adding another
matrix, our matrix becomes full rank, which is easier to invert (e.g.: it allows us to
have a ZF equalizer).
We really needs to remember this: LOS tends to not be full rank (which is the message
behind a cryptic line in the slides)!!

Detour on importance of channel modeling
The main idea is that there are plenty channel models; there are many for whichever
application, not only wireless.
Of all the models, some are coming from measurements, some are coming from a
theoretical modeling. We try to model reality, but we don't have a clear and easy
picture of it; this is true not only for digital communications, but also for wireless
communications in general. This also applies to fibers!
The fiber is connected to the FTTC, Fiber To The Cabinet; there's a sort of box

We need to have a modeling such as the one we saw even for fiber optics networks.
The twisted pairs can be considered as MIMO (which is to say: we don't have MIMO just
for wireless communications): the receiver on one side will get interference from the
other cable!

ADSL is MIMO as well! For this specific application we don't do Zero Forcing, though: we
use a technology called vectoring (this came from the american company ASSIA): you
have copper twisted pair, which by the way cost a lot to change. What we try to do in
this case is that since we know that we have a MIMO system, we try to send the

signals in such a way that in the end we have no interference between the cables; it's
called vectoring because we look at vectorial quantities and try to align them.
The FTTC is asymmetric because we have much more information on the left (between a
main transmitter and the cabinet) than on the right (between the cabinet and the
houses). We're sending info to the houses in a coordinated way; this does not hold for
communications from the houses.
​Mynote
In the sense that while towards the cabinet we can coordinate the signals which will
then be demultiplexed towards the various houses, we can't do the same between
the cabinet and the houses.

This is to say that even modelling a channel such as an ADSL channel is as painful as it
would be to model a wireless channel.
Exam
The exam will be written, but it's reserved to also have an oral exam.
We might be asked to have an oral exam if theoretical and practical parts are really
different from one another.
We might say: "I think I deserve a better grade"; we need to motivate this and say
"I want to show I'm well prepared". In this case, there's no safety net: our grade
can as well decrease!
Professor will be available to explain what happened in the exam.

Modeling of MIMO channels
Introduction: space/time decoupling; analytical and parametric
models
Whether the separation of large and small scale phenomena still holds depends on the
physical size of the antenna arrays
08.3_pp_131_208_Channel_modeling, page 46
Unless otherwise stated, we consider that the number of antennas and their physical
spacing are such that the array size does not exceed the size of the local scattering
neighborhood. Under this premise, which may only need revisiting in the realm of
massive MIMO, the separation is upheld—with the addition of antenna correlation as a
small-scale parameter that should be made dependent, as appropriate, on the largescale channel gain. The largescale models presented earlier apply verbatim and, in
the sequel, we focus exclusively on the small-scale domain via stochastic modeling.

The book then highlights that we're still using the normalization

2

E[||H|| F ] = E[tr(HH
= E[tr(H

∗

∗

)]

(2.86)

H)]

= Nt Nr .

even though in many models we have that every entry of H is unit-power.
It's also true that we'll still have the large-scale term √G.
08.3_pp_131_208_Channel_modeling, page 46
In contrast with SISO, where we found it convenient to bind space and time, in MIMO
they need to be decoupled. The space dimension, properly sampled, becomes the
antenna dimension, and it is unlocked from the time selectivity engendered by
motion. More precisely, the actual trajectory of a transceiver, which induces the
time selectivity, needs to be distinguished from the segments connecting every
antenna pair (see Fig. 3.22).

Fig. 3.22
Here, as well as with SISO, we have two types of models:
Analytical: they concern themselves with the modeling of the joint distribution of
entries, assuming that time/frequency dynamics are modeled in SISO.
While this disregards dependencies which may exist in reality, it allows to emphasize
the MIMO nature of the link.
According to professor, they are narrowband models able to capture spatial
characteristics:
They're used primarily for analysis, to develop intuition about the role of multiple
antennas
They're inspired by reality, but they're not a direct physical description of
propagation
Parametric: they offer the possibility of capturing the space, time, and frequency
dimensions jointly. According to professor, are more general models with several
parameters
They may also be defined for time and frequency variation
Most of the standard models are in parametric form
Analytical models are useful when we want to establish the capacity: "what happens
when we put as the input a value with Gaussian distribution?"; this is not realistic,

because a Gaussian goes from −∞ to +∞, which is not feasible. We use that model to
see what the maximum is.
We use some models which are not a direct physical description of propagation.
Paramteric models are even more general.

Analytical models
Kronecker model
This model is harder to treat (when compared to the models we previously considered),
because we'd need the machinery of tensor calculus in order to handle it, which is not
something we know.
We thus pass from a 4-d tensor to a matrix generated from stacking the components of
the tensor. Point is this is generally not tractable.
We then have the Kronecker model: the channel entries are jointly Gaussian and they're
thus fully captured by a second-order statistical description. We can hastily consider
this description as a 4d tensor
′

′

R H (i, j; i , j ) = [R r ] i,i ′ [R t ] j,j ′ ,

(3.113)

with the first term being the receive correlation and the second term being the transmit
correlation.
This can only be done if it is separable. The model there is capturing from the theoretical
POV the spatial correlation.
08.3_pp_131_208_Channel_modeling, page 49
The significance of (3.113) is the following: the correlation between the signal
fading at any two receive antennas is the same irrespective of the transmit antenna
from which the signal emanates, and conversely the correlation between the fading
of the signals emanating from any two transmit antennas is the same at any receive
antenna [277]. Intuitively, this requires that the local scattering processes around
the transmitter and the receiver be decoupled.

For completeness
(3.113)

is actually a simplified form of the complete description
′

∗

′

R H (i, j; i , j ) = E[[H − μ H ] i,j [H − μ H ] i ′ ,j ′ ] ;

(3.111)

the simplified form is the pivotal modeling element of the Kronecker model, which is
also referred to as the separable or product correlation model.

Under the simplification (3.113), the channel matrix can be expressed as

Square root of matrix
We see that H has an average and then the square root of a matrix. The root of a
matrix is such that multiplied by its square root will give itself.
We know that this matrix is semidefinite positive if we have a matrix such that:

This can generally be used in order to understand power consumption, which is linked
to the stability of multidimensional systems.
We want this to be positive, since when we want to have the square root of
something greater or equal to 0.

When we'll use it, we'll see it in more detail.
08.3_pp_131_208_Channel_modeling, p.180
Unless otherwise stated, throughout this text we consider the correlation matrices to
be nonsingular, not only because that is appropriate from the perspective of modeling
situations of interest, but further because dimensions in the null space of any
singular correlation matrix could be taken out, leaving a reduced-dimension
nonsingular model. The nonsingularity of the correlation matrices has the welcome
benefit of yielding channel matrices H that are themselves full-rank with probability
1, a property that prevents certain nuisances in the formulation of subsequent
chapters.

We have a narrow angle from where the signals are coming; at the same time, the
signals see the antenna without scattering. This is because the antennas are high: up
there, we have air but no scatterers.
This was a good design principle for when we don't use MIMO (2G, 3G). We know that
scattering etc are not negative per se when we do MIMO: they're needed in order to
have a full rank matrix.
It might happen that the matrix gets full rank only because there's scattering in a local
neighborhood of the receiver (I think!!!).
In the book's words:
08.3_pp_131_208_Channel_modeling, p.180
A common situation in cellular systems is to have a base station elevated above the
clutter, deprived of local scattering, communicating with a user located within the
clutter. Then, the user’s PAS can be modeled as Clarke–Jakes, with the consequent
applicability of the correlation function in Example 3.8. In turn, the base station’s
PAS can be assumed to be highly localized around its mean, μ ; as shown in Fig.
3.23, μ is dictated by the azimuth location of the user (relative, without loss of
θ

θ

generality, to the endfire direction) while the spread is determined by the angle
subtended, from the vantage of the base station, by the local scattering
neighborhood around the user. This spread depends on the elevation and the
distance, among other aspects, and it can be as small as a few degrees.

Sometimes, MIMO in 2 directions works differently. Here we must respect our rule of

the base stations are really separated, because we have small wavelengths.

Example 3.38 (crude approximation of antenna correlation):
exponential antenna correlation model
This is an example to take into account; we talk about parametric channel models since
here we have just a ρ: guessing what that it is not simple, but it is easy to deal with.
Quoting what the book said, as an alternative to the computation of ^bd93a5 and
^829d33, we can choose an arbitrary correlation function (as long as it decays with
distance); while this might not correspond to an actual physical interpretation, it can
suffice for some purposes (such as crude preliminary analyses).

We select this model because we know how to deal with it; this is a typical engineering
tradeoff.
This is useful because a channel model can give us an idea of what is happening: we
have the Kronecker model and we've found a way to model it purely by our own
(detached from reality) parameters.

About research internship and PhDs
Note
Research internship: it generally does not help enter PhDs.
What we should do is visit rn the PhD school website. How do they select the PhD
students?
We apply, we receive a grade given by a committee; the maximum is 100 and then a
decreasing order. Depending on the amount of money given by the state, the first
10/12 are getting the salary from the state, which is equal for everyone. Then some
other people coming from the budget of university of Padova. Then we have
companies or institutions financing some things.
If we apply for telecommunications we're free to do our PhD in whatever we want in
the area of telecommunications.
The rank from companies have the research of the student limited by what is written
in the rank: I want somebody to study the MIMO model for THz communications.
There is a ranking; how do we make it? The ranking is based on an:
Oral colloquio
Past records
Project
When we submit the application, we submit the project, what we want to
investigate.
Usually, if you talk with a professor, you can decide on a project (might give a huge
advantage!). Here we're looking at both the grades at the Bachelor's and Master's.
Generally, if we're below 27 as an average, it's really difficult to get accepted.
In the oral exam, the committee is asking about the project, to investigate the
probability of success.
The CV is important as well! We need to achieve evidence that we achieved things.
We can apply for both the open ranks or for a specific rank.
The companies are kept out of the selection process!
If someone decides to not take the role, everyone shifts up; it's important to have

good records, to write a good project and to be able to defend the project. In the
CV, it's important to prove things with evidence.
We can put at most 3 letters of reference: we can ask a reference letter to whoever
we want; generally this should be done with professor who know us very well.
Letters of references can be put together and are confronted at the end of our PhD.
It's smart to use the research internship to prepare for the thesis! It might be super
good to publish a paper before graduating, it might put us apart from the rest of the
group.

UIU model
08.3_pp_131_208_Channel_modeling, page 52
Although the Kronecker correlation model has been shown to fit the data reasonably
well in numerous instances, both indoors [272, 282–284] and outdoors [285], it also
has limitations that stem from the transmit and receive PAS being fully decoupled, a
condition that tends to result in optimistic assessments of capacity or of to-beintroduced metrics such as diversity [286–290]. A modeling representation that,
while still short of offering the complete representation in (3.111) or (3.112), is
more general than the Kronecker model is

∗

H = μ H + U r H ind U t

where U , U are fixed unitary matrices, while H
r

t

ind

,

(3.133)

is made of zero-mean independent

non-identically distributed (IND) entries, which are generally Gaussian (if this is the
case, this is called the Weichselberger model). This is another model which generalizes
the Kronecker model, by the way.
We put random independent values in H

ind

. We'll see that this is useful. Is it realistic? It

depends!
08.3_pp_131_208_Channel_modeling, page 52
As its main feature, the UIU model allows for the transmit and receive PAS to be
coupled, something that becomes clearer once the model is further specialized in the
subsequent section

It can be shown that thanks to the writing of (3.133) we're actually writing the Karhunen–
Loève expansion of H , which eventually gives
c

unitary.

our job is done.

Parametric models
Ray-based model
We don't touch millimeter waves; for the millimeter waves, we see that the channel is
very much concentrated on a certain angle of departure and a certain direction of arrival.
This is because mmwave is really directional: we're not spreading, we have very few
angles.
It's useful to adopt a ray-tracing model in order to handle mmwaves, since they are
somewhat like light; ray-tracing is the prevalent approach to the analysis of such
waves: this is a parametric model!
We have that (thanks to the reasoning at 08.3_pp_131_208_Channel_modeling, page 58)
for a ray-based model the (i, j)th entry of H is given by
Q−1

neighborhoods.

Speedrun of various models (a bit more focus to the SalehValenzuela model)
​What's in this section?
Professor really wanted to be done with this chapter, thus he speedran across
various different models. Unless they are actually used somewhere else, I'll just
write here what we said in class.

One-ring model: not even mentioned in class.
Two-ring model: we get the same Clarke-Jake PAS.
Clustered models: in the book this is explained very well.

We cluster in the sense that we take the delay in ns and we also take the angle from
which the ray departs (DoD); often, we see there are delays and angles which are
grouped together: we try to reunite them and try to model using those angles and
delays.
This is really useful as it allows for a simplification which is pertinent with what is
actually happening.

The really important picture is 3.17, which is not in the slides: that is what we should
remember.

Fig. 3.17
Even though in class professor said nothing about that figure, we see that it is the
representation of a multi-carrier transmission, in which we have time selectivity (reason
why we have T , see ^ecd663) and frequency selectivity (reason why we have B , which
is sadly not well defined).
c

c

3.16: power profile of the Saleh-Valenzuela model. The exponent according to which we
see the decay is pretty high! We then see we have a jump etc etc.
This picture is much better to understand the model.

Fig. 3.16
The idea is that the modelers noticed that rays are distributed in clusters; in a way such
that:
In a cluster, the power of the rays decays exponentially with exponent γ
In general, the power of the rays decays exponentially with exponent Γ

SV

SV

Each ray is then Rayleigh-faded.
The τ value in fig 3.16 indicates the lead ray of the k-th cluster.
k

Some other minor info can be found at 08.3_pp_131_208_Channel_modeling, page 33.
About polarization: we have 2 independent polarizations, giving us two orthogonal
functions. The one which is more used is the horizontal one. In general, we have a
helixoid around which the polarization goes: it is generally not oriented only along one
axis.

In general, this is useful if we have something point-to-point. We can't tell people to
keep phones oriented in one specific way, but we can design fixed systems in order to
support this.

Moore-Penrose Pseudoinverse detour: pseudoinverse applied to
systems of equations
Professor would like to go a little bit back on why we consider the Moore-Penrose
pseudoinverse, as there are many different ways to look at that.
We call it Moore-Penrose because there's another more general pseudoinverse. Professor
would like to give some intuition on the Moore-Penrose pseudoinverse.
In general, when we have a linear system such as
Ax = b ,

which cases could we have (btw, we don't restrict ourselves to a square matrix)?
We might have a fat matrix and a vector

⎢⎥

how many solutions will we have in such a situation? Let's make an example:
1

]
6

= [ ] ,
8

x 1 + 2x 2 + 3x 3 = 7
4x 1 + 5x 2 + 6x 3 = 8

it's clear that we have infinite solutions.

:

The Moore-Penrose is really parsimonious; if we take the MP pseudoinverse and we
multiply that with b, we'll find the smallest vector which satisfies the system Ax = b.
​Elaborating

So the idea is that we basically get the Moore-Penrose pseudoinverse K and thanks
to its properties, we'll have that x = Kb is the smallest vector solving
Ax = b .

The MP inverse, if we use it, will give the vector x with the smallest Euclidean norm.
We decided to use this inverse because we might have problems in which we have
infinite solutions and we need to select one: we need to select the solution for which
the x vector has the minimum euclidean norm; this is generally because we want to
spend the least amount of possible energy.

We may have another situation, in which the matrix is tall!
1

This case generally ends up having more equations than variables; what we might do
here is instead of finding something solving Ax = b, we find something solving
Ax = c

with c which minimizes the norm

||c − b||

:

we're solving a LS problem! We're selecting the vector which is closest to the solution.

Automatically, the Moore-Penrose inverse is doing the job for us, selecting x , x so that
1

2

the multiplication Ax gives a value which is as near as possible to the solution.
This as well is engineering-core.
This is why the Moore-Penrose should be tought at linear algebra course.
If it is invertible, then we know how to find things deterministically; if we have a
situation such as the last, we'd normally just say that we can't solve it. The MP
instead takes the best approximation!
Demonstrating this is not a walk in the park: the real demonstration should show that
there are the left hand side and right hand side; we'd need to show that they are
equivalent etc etc.
We can look at it on the moodle.

​Some extra considerations
Nonetheless, we're considering two different estimations of the same channel; it
thus makes sense to subtract the two values and have them be 0. It's as if we
said: we take a coin toss r ∼ B(0.5) and we define X = r + 1 and Y = r + 2; then,
since we're considering the same toss (just like how here we're considering the
same realization of the channel), we get that X − R = −1 deterministically.

About ergodicity
Ergodicity happens when the expectation is equal to the time domain average

Stationarity in mean means that
E[x(ω, t)] = μ ,

with no dependence on time.
If we have ergoditicity, we have that
T

1
∫
2T

x(ω, t) dt = μ = E[x(ω, t)] ,
−T

which means we have ergodicity in mean!

Not all processes are ergodic! Sometimes to have ergodic processes we need to
remove everything which is predictable (such as sinusoids and similar things!).
For example, if we have a process such as
x(t) = A sin(ωt + ϕ)

with ϕ ∼ U (0, 2π), this is a process, but is it really random?
Mathematicians don't care about this, but for us engineer this is important.
Depending on the realization we take, if we take a point and we know A, we can
predict all the future of that realization.
With an ergodic process we can only guess something close, but we should not be
able to predict something about the present or past.

Single-user SISO
Today we'll start with chapter 4.

Introduction: capacity of AWGN channel, plotting p ,
various writings of C , total channel gain
e

This chapter is revolving around the formula of capacity, trying to understand the
relation between the various fundamental values.

We know we have that an AWGN channel has a capacity of
C(SNR) = log 2 (1 + SNR)

achieved by using Gaussian distributed codewords.
In this chapter we'll see waterfilling and about the frequency selective channels.
We'll look at the capacity if the transmitter knows the channel information or if only
receiver does or if both do.
We may be using OFDM and not know the channel: we'll use equal power for every
carrier; of course if we use OFDM we at least need to know the lenght of the channel,
as we'll need to set the cyclic prefix length accordingly. The energy we spend for the
cyclic prefix is kind of wasted: we use it to simplify our relations, but it is wasted.
On top of that, if we know the channel we can distribute the power across the
subcarriers in an optimum way. This is why we use pilots: using them, we can estimate
the channel. This as well can be seen as a waste, since we could be transmitting
information; point is that then the receiver gets to know the channel; if the receiver
feeds the info of the channel back to the transmitter, then the transmitter can optimize
itself.
From the last chapter we know the RMS of the channel; we generally interpret that to be
Gaussian. We can then make some assumptions and verify if they're true (starting with
equal power) and then we'll have a return channel used to gather feedback from the
receiver.
This feedback loop is crucial if we want to get the most out of OFDM. Another thing is
that the feedback channel is such that we send some information related to the channel
itself.
It's also important to take into account the fact that we'll need to quantize our channel:
how many bits should we use to quantize the channel? This is an open problem!
There are many papers discussing this; the amount of bits used are really variable.
Usually we have an idea, we implement and measure the performance: the difference is
called implementation gap. We'll be left with a high probability of error, which will be in
dB.
The simple idea used in practical system in order to measure the error in dB is to have a
curve such as:

We select a certain value and then we measure a distance, which is in dB:

And then how do we implement the return channel?

We can't assume that the return channel is error-free, of course.
We need to have a reliable link; having probability of error equal to 0 means we'd need
to have a code with a block length which is very large (actually, infinite). Point is that
such a code would give a pretty large delay, while our problem is also that we'd like to
have a small delay: if we have too much, by the time we deliver info about the channel,
it has already changed.
Normally in the books this is forgotten, but it's important: we need to change this so
that we're bringing not-too-much service information around; if we don't, we're not
bringing any user information.

We're mostly interested about the exchange of billing information.

We could see many formulations of the formula of capacity; we can find
1
C =
2

the

1
2

log 2 (1 + SNR) [bits/use ↔ bits/s/Hz] ;

depends on whether we're considering the channel as complex (no 1/2) or not

(has 1/2). We have no B in front of the equation before; without B is the capacity per
channel use; we suppose we use the channel with an interval T = 1/B, meaning that the
rate at which we use the channel is B.
This is why we might also find
C = B log 2 (1 + SNR) [bits/s] .

Let's then consider
log 2 (1 + SNR)

:

which is the range for SNR? Normally it is ∈ [0, ∞).
We might as well express it as dB: the SNR can get really close to 0, meaning that for
SNR = 0, we get a capacity of 0.
We of course have
SNR| dB = 10 log 10 SNR .

Of course if in dB we can have a negative value, meaning that the signal has less power
than the noise.
We need to measure the SNR at the point of decision; there are systems working at
negative SNR in dB, meaning that the power of the noise is much higher than the power
of the signal; what is the use case? Military use! In the army we don't want the enemy
to get the message, even if it is encrypted; if my transmission is detected, the radar
can detect where I am (we just have 2 receivers which can understand the location by
looking at the power while they rotate). We don't even want the enemy to know we're
transmitting. If we stay below a certain transmission noise, we can accomplish this!
LORA is such that if we transmit very far, the power goes below the level of the noise.
If we've seen a different kind of modulation, we see that we can also work on the other
side; the way to do this is to use DSSS (Direct Sequence Spread Spectrum): we have a
+1,-1 signal and we multiply this with a pseudo-random sequence

1

-1
Pseudo-random sequence

We're spreading this signal to a larger spectrum, eventually going below the level of
noise. How do I recover the signal? I multiply again by the +1, -1 we had before, getting

back the original signal. We get back to a small bandwidth: we have again sufficient SNR
for reliable transmission.
This is just a silly PAM, but we could apply it to more elaborate trasmissions.

We know the SNR at which a certain model/protocol will work!
We look at the SNR at the point in which we'll transmit; we don't care about what
happens in the rest of the channel.

POTS and telephone system detour
POTS (telephone system; we then had ISDN (Integrated System Digital Network);
nowadays we only have the IP network); even in the IP network we have control traffic:
we need to exchange info about the routing tables.
About fixed phones
An example: we pick up the phone; we hear the tone, which means that the line is
available. Some time ago, it could happen to not hear the tone of three line, but the
busy line tone. We're using a protocol in this exchange of information. Once we get
the line, we start making the number; the network will understand when we're
finished writing. Then there's a dialogue between various nodes to understand where
the person we're calling is.
If the other person is found, their phone starts ringing. Only when the other person
picks up, the user data starts. Once the phone is put on hook, there's another
signal saying the communication has been closed.
Mobile phones use a different protocols: they don't know when we've finished
putting the number. This is why we have the green number.
At the time we had a phone sending impulses to the local switch; when the phone
which turned sent an impulse at every tick while coming back. If we pressed 9,
for example, there was just 1 tick.
The one with the buttons understood the numbers in another way; it still was
connected to the central exchange; info was covered using the DTMF (Dual Tone
MultiFrequency); we have various different frequencies: depending on what we
press, we'll get a different transmitted signal. The tones (=frequencies) are such
to give the coordinates of the number that we want to call.
The prefix code is what's used for the communication, actually. If we have a
double 0 at the start, the network understands we want to go outside of italy; by
pressing 00 we express the fact that we want to go somewhere else. We then
have country codes: 3,4 mean europe. Italy is 039 : of course we can't have a

country with a prefix of

0391 ,

because otherwise italy's wouldn't be valid

anymore.
If we have 039049 this means we want to contact Padova.
There must be a worldwide agreement on the numbers. The CEPT is the one
assignining the prefixes.

That of the phone was the first example of standardization; we cannot do this with the
mobile phone since mobile phones are digital; it would be a nightmare to find codes
related to mobile networks: they are ISDN numbers. In the mobile network there's
another hidden number which allows us to (for example) change operator.
If we send a message, sending a number one by one would definitely congest the
network. It was preferred to have the green button and send the message all at once.
Random story on the africa linking between the satellitar and terrestrial network;
terrible fiasco rip.
We need to have a function which should inform the network about the location of a
certain phone; if an italian phone is in egypt, network contacts the number; HRR is
contacted and that register knows where you are.
We can then transfer our request to connect to the register network.
The operator does not know where we are exactly, as this would be too much
information to hold; the cell is too much informaiton as well; they did a location area,
which is a set of cells such that if our cell is in there and not engaged in a call: how
many cells there are depends on our network design; the location area is written in the
HRR: when a call reaches us, the cell broadcasts information: it tells which people are
requested to connect every certain amount of milliseconds; the phone understands that
message and if it is requested to connect to a call, it starts the transmission. The
resource is so scarse that we only allocate it when we know that the transmission is
going well.
The network keeps track of what's happening in order to send the bill; we then need
to read the bills and understand if the person was at a certain location or not.

Infineon detour
Infineon detour; he talked about what infineon does and what they sell to who.
Aurix: architecture designed for cars; this is needed since the interrupts need to be
served really quickly. Their processors range in various power values. The ones who
made Aurix are mostly focused in analog, they develop the peripherals for the car.
They are developing telecom systems: one example is the mandatory system which
detects how many people are in the car when we close it. More than that, we also need
to sense the environment and whether the driver is fully awake.
Another example are the sensors which detect whether we're drunk.
Infineon is a very good place to work: they take care of people for their growth; salaries
are aligned with the international market; open positions are first broadcast to internal
people. We then have a lot of project management: when working in automotive it's
needed to design things really carefully.

They also need to comply with many regulations.
Infineon uses machine learning in order to ensure a really good production yield (e.g.:
they use ML to have a frequency as close as possible to the desired one).
We can also decide whether we want to continue with a technical career or a
managerial career.
What is the structure of the exam?
First of all, we might be checked on the background.
The first part is closed book. We can have 1A4 paper which must have our name and
student id; we can only write on the front!!
At the start of the exam, all our cheat sheets will be put on the desk and we won't
be able to look at them. We'll then need to answer to ten questions in a crocette
format. We then have a theoretical exercise: example "prove that the MMSE
estimator is ...": there are no numbers here! The first exercise is a theoretical
exercise. We'll have 2 sets of papers; when we decide we have finished, we can
deposit the theoretical part and pick up the cheat sheet: we'll then have a numerical
exercise, in which there will be numbers.
We of course need to put together our sequence number.
We can also take a calculator with us, but it shall not be connected to the internet.

How to reduce the energy per bit
How do we reduce the energy per bit? We increase the transmission frequency or we
lower the power; if I transmit 10 bits per second and consider 20 bits per second,
each bit has less energy. If we increase the bits per second, then we have lower
power.

Detour on the block scheme of a telecom system+history of
modulation
Block scheme, channel coding rate and measuring channel coding
performances

POSSIBLE QUESTIONS FOR THE EXAM
In the exam professor might ask us to explain why complex exponentials are
eigenvalues of the filters.
A reccommendation is to reharse OFDM.
Another theoretical question might be to explain the OFDM modulation.

History of coding; we've been talking about the Shannon limit; in order to achieve
capacity, we need to input AWGN signals; problem is that we don't know how to put
information in the AWGN signals.
In communications systems we have a source of information; what we do is that we try
to get rid of all the redundancy, which is called source coding and then we have bits:

Of course source coding is lossless; just as an example, jpeg is lossy.
Let's say we have a 8 ⋅ 10 pixels which we multiply by 3 (3 channels) multiplied by 16
and then by 60; by multiplying them together, we reach
6

9

≃ 23 ⋅ 10 Gbps ,

meaning that we'd need to transmit a lot of data.
There are algorithms which dramatically reduce this; for example, if we have a picture
of a person, we use both the redundancy in the space: we find equal regions and say
they're of the same color. We only transmit the difference in time, dramatically
reducing the bitrate.
Here the encoding can be lossless, so that we don't miss anything. For sounds .FLAC is
not losing information, while .mp3 is losing information which our ears don't hear. What
source coding does to a screen is to have lossless compression; the next thing we do is
channel coding, which adds some extra redundancy in a controlled way so that we're
able to recover errors made by the channel.

Of course we also have that each block has blocks in it (in the sense that each step of

our block scheme can be further developed!).
Example: Trellis coded modulation puts together channel coding and modulation to
reach a better optimum.
Now they also try to do source and channel coding together; they actually try to do that
together with machine learning. The receiver will learn how to make a model.
For now, though, we'll stick with a simple model.
Pretty soon, people froze modulation: there are lots of different modulations; same for
the demodulator.
Research has been for a lot on channel coding and decoding: we reached block coding;
we take K bits and map them to N bits, with N > K . The rate of the code is actually
K/N . For example, we have a rate
if we doubled the number of bits: intuitively, the
1
2

bits after channel coding have half of the information.
If we want to understand how well channel coding is working, we need to plot the
information we have before channel coding in function of probability of error.

The channel coding on the left is better, with respect to the one on the right; we also
need to remember that capacity is for reliable communication: the probability of error
should be as low as we want. Generally our target is 10 ; we can then look at how
many bits we need in order to have what in an engineering way we consider an error−9

free transmission.

History of modulations (+hard/soft decoding)
*Professor shows history of modulations*. Voyager: they had two cores, one working
and the other receiving updates for the other.
Soft decoding: we give the probability that the bit is 0 or 1 and then we operate on the
probabilities.
Convolutional codes: the code is a convolution in the Galois fields (which is a discrete
space); LDPC is from Gallagher, but the complexity of decoding the LDPC was so high
that it was dropped.
1967: Viterbi decoder. Concatenated codes were also invented (we concatenate a block
for random errors and one for past errors).
Turbo codes were a really big jump: they get 1dB from the capacity. Turbo had a
principle of filling the decoder etc etc; at this point people were saying we're done!:
we were just a fraction of dBs from the Shannon limit, but then tech was ready for

implementing LDPC codes, which had large blocks; they were implemented in the 1996
but they were invented in 1960.
Hard decoding means that we have the demodulator which outputs either 0 or 1; instead,
soft decoding outputs a number which is ∈ [−1, 1], which is (through appropriate
calculations) the probability that P [b = 0] ∈ [0, 1]. If we have 0.1, it's more likely to be 1
etc etc.
If we use soft decoding, we're not sure whether we have 0 or 1: it's not a firm belief
of what we have. If we know nothing, we output 0.5. Soft decoding has a curve which is
much closer to the Shannon bound when compared to that of Hard coding.
If we push the power, then the difference becomes almost irrelevant.
Then, polar codes were invented, using a mathematical technique called polarization; he
got the demodulator using a channel which is more and more polarized towards 0 and 1,
discarding what's in the middle. Polar codes reach similar performance as turbo codes,
but with smaller blocks: this means that they're more useful when we have smaller
blocks. This was a kind of history of decoding, real coding. If we don't know where the
limit is, we don't know how good we are.
Constructive theorem: tells us how to do something; non-constructive: tells us
something exists.
Capacity theory has 2 problems:
1. It's not constructive: we don't know how to achieve the bound
2. In the demonstration we don't put any limit on how big is the block of bits we're
transmitting: it's working in a so-called infinite regime
In order to reach the Shannon bound we need to have infinite (or very large) blocks of
bits.
Guy established a bound which is at page 221;
1

2

V = (1 −
(1 + SNR)

(which is different from what is handled in the Shannon scenario) the capacity will be
V

less than what we'd have with infinite block length; the difference from the infinite
block length scenario is of V (defined in (4.66)).

Block scheme for 2G voice calls
Let's keep the block model from before:

there are many problems left unsolved, still.
Source coding and decoding are almost always working separately (from the rest of the
blocks): we just start after the source coding and end before the source decoding:

we do so because we can just assume we're transmitting bits.
This is not always true: let's consider 2G; at the time, the vocoder (=coding for the
voice) could only send something like 9.6Kbit/s: we don't get a nice voice at that rate, if
we're not smart. GSM actually made a mathematical model; this is a kind of box with
some rules so from the voice, the box (source coding) was extracting 3 things:
The mathematical model
The input, aka the voice; this was done every 20ms: this is what we give to the
mathematical model which is as close as possible to the emitted sound; we're not
able to completely match that; thus we give the next point
The residue, what we'd need to add to what we otherwise would have: this allows
for a good message

This allowed to transmit much less data. The thing we cannot afford to lose is the
mathematical model; the input taken every 20ms does not change too much: we might
want to replay the previous input; if we don't add the residue, we get a metallic sort of
voice (we may have heard it: it's the mathematical model without the residue).
What we do if we have to make a system is basically that since we have 3 different
things, we have that source coding will have 3 outputs

Also, we're doing cyclic redundancy check, in a way such that each block is

We get A, compute f (A) and compare it with what we got. An example of f (A) is the
hash: this is needed because if we decode the residue wrong, we might as well throw it
away.
Normally the decoder and the channel decoder are simplified; there are things missing,
such as synchronization: how can I understand where's the correct time for sampling
the signal? Who tells me that? There is a complete topic which is synchronization both in
time and in frequency: we should have a reference for both time and frequency. For this
as well there's a huge amount of studies for sequencies; usually we have CAZAC
(Constant Amplitude Zero AutoCorrelation waveform). CAZAC appends a piece of a
sequence and then cross correlates; when we have a peak, we know we're aligned.
How we get aligned in frequency? What if my oscillator is not 2GHz like I wanted? We

have to measure the accuracy of oscillators in parts per million (n [PPM] means that
every million Hertz, we're deviating of n [Hz]): if we're shifted of ±10Hz, we're not
baseband when we have a baseband signal: this is all done in synchronization.

Question: how do we handle two signals which are delayed
replicas of one another?
Replica signals, if I have a certain bandwidth, we need to have impulses spaced at least
; this goes back to the Fourier analysis; if we have a bandwidth B we have duality
between time and frequency; a signal of bandwidth B can at most change every T . If the
1

B

= T

pulses are too close, then we're violating this law, making it difficult to tell them
apart. Everything is a matter of equalization, though (example: successive interference
equalization).
In the fiber optics, if we send a pulse at the beginning, we then have dispersion which
gets more and more intense over time: we then need to regenerate the payload (he's
actually talking about chromatic dispersion).
Even in fiber optics we have a lot of channel coding and synchronization. Once people
studied the channel models for fiber optics, but no more now.
Instead, discriminating signals sent by different users is handled by MAC, which
determines for example power sharing; how we mix together many users is a problem
which is being handled right now.

A problem is we might have many users transmitting to satellites; even if the satellite is
low orbit, it is illuminating a big part of earth; there will be many users transmitting in
an uncoordinated way. It's difficult to receive and demodulate many users. There are
standards and such to do this.
The last lesson will be reserved for a mockup exam; we'll see how it works in order
to have it clear how the exam works.

which is greater than 0! Below −1.59dB we can't have reliable communication.

We must remember two things about those:
1. As we go with SNR → 0 or SNR|

dB

, the spectral efficiencies go down; as we

→ −∞

said, we can reach Shannon's capacity only if we use a Gaussian input; we're never
able to use Gaussian inputs though. What we can do is to use for example QAM. We
can then ask how the various modulations are behaving: when we go down with the
SNR, all modulations perform really badly, as they get squeezed. They achieve
capacity, in an engineering way of talking. We see that we can't distinguish the
various curves anymore, though. Problem is that even if we achieved capacity, we
can only transmit very little information.
2. SNR = 0 means that the power of the noise is equal to the power of the signal; if
we increase the power over a certain level, we don't get more capacity. With BPSK,
dB

we cannot transmit more than 1 bit per symbol! This is the capacity given the
constellation; there's a hard limit we cannot go above of. In the picture we have
more reliability as we increase the power.
Normally we decide a certain probability of error and then we plot how many bits we can
transmit per interval per probability of error. This can change slightly by changing the
value of the P we're using as our target.
Increasing the SNR diminishes the probability of error, but does not increase the
e

capacity.
If we use BPSK, we know that at 1MHz we can at most transmit 1Mbps; let's say we
then switch to QPSK; we'll then have 2Mbps.
This is clear from the graph above here. If we're at low SNR, we don't really care about
the modulation; if we have more bandwidth, we can work with that! Depending on the
SNR we have and other things, we can choose a different modulation.

There's also an exercise: what does it mean that the input should be as similar as
possible to a Gaussian? If we plot the contour of a Gaussian, we'll have

Other modulations such as the APSK emulate the Gaussian much better.
We need to respect the fact that in order to reach capacity, we need to be Gaussian.
The graph on the right is a bit less important: we see similar behaviors and that at
−1.59dB we cannot transmit anything. We see that BPSK flattens much before: using the
same energy per bit, we don't get the most out of the other constellations; the higher
the order of the constellations, the more energy we need in order to reach the plateau.

Up to 15 years ago, the capacity was though to be only log (1 + SNR), but in the
demonstration which leads us to that value there are several assumptions; one of those
2

assumptions is that we can increase the length of the block to an infinite value; the
capacity is the limit obtained by pushing the length of the block towards infinity. We'd
thus need push towards infinity the block before transmitting through the AWGN channel.
This is not often put clear to people.
In order to reach capacity log (1 + SNR) we need to have the possibility to use blocks of
bits which are as large as we want; these are infinite blocks of bits and in reality we'll
2

never be able to transmit such large blocks.
No one was able to answer the question: "how much am I loosing with respect to
capacity by having a finite length?", until Yuri Polianski did; he said that the spectral

efficiency in the finite block length regime is the usual capacity, with the exception that
we lose something, given by the second term in (4.67); then we have a function with SNR
, probability of error p . There is a factor there which is a penalty for using a certain
e

finite block length.

We can observe that:
In the low-SNR regime, C(SNR) is—to first order—linear in the SNR. Every doubling of
the received power roughly doubles the capacity. The communication is therefore
power-efficient.
In the high-SNR regime, C(SNR) is only logarithmic in the SNR. Every doubling yields
only one additional b/s/Hz. The communication is power-inefficient.
