Sneak preview of link adaptation
08.4_pp_209_294_Single-user_SISO, page 18
Mobile wireless transceivers feature a number of constellations; rarely BPSK
(because it wastes half of each complex dimension in baseband or, equivalently, half
the passband bandwidth), customarily QPSK, 16-QAM, 64-QAM, and, for evolved
releases of LTE as well as for NR and for certain WLANs, even 256-QAM. Very-shortrange or fixed wireless transceivers could feature even denser constellations. The
combination of these various constellations with a restricted number of binary coding
rates yields a set of discrete spectral efficiencies, each termed a Modulation and
Coding Scheme (MCS), at which the transmitter can operate. It is important for the
set of MCSs to cover the entire range of operational interest, more or less uniformly.
The transmitter needs to select, depending on the SNR, the most appropriate MCS. In
a canonical AWGN channel, this needs to be done only once. In fading channels,
alternatively, it becomes a recurring procedure, termed link adaptation (alternatively
adaptive modulation and coding or rate control), that constitutes one of the
hallmarks of modern communication. With ideal coding and N → ∞, the MCS to select

would be the highest MCS supported by the channel, i.e., the one whose nominal
spectral efficiency is largest while not exceeding I (SNR) for the corresponding
constellation. With practical codes, necessarily finite in length and not error-free, it
is more convenient to select the MCS that yields the largest possible throughput.
Erroneous codewords are then subject to hybridARQ and, if necessary, to higher-layer
retransmissions to ensure that all payload bits are correctly delivered in the end.
This breaks down the responsibility for the reliability of the transmission between the
PHY layer and the rest of the protocol stack [361].
We can explain link adaptation through some specific graphs; let's say we're in a real
deployment. We need to serve a certain number of users

Depending on where the user is, we can have a different SNR and thus capacity. We then
have a tradeoff between fairness and capacity. If we promised 1Mbps, we'll have to
pump the power high. The closer user will have extra power which is unused effectively.
What we might do is to have a fixed choice for the distant user, like "we use BPSK",
giving them 1Mbps at 1MHz; we can use something else for the other so that they have
better spectral efficiency. We're the ones deciding how to divide resources.
All of this depends on the service level agreement, which is where 5G failed completely.
What's the point then? It's that any real system is changing the MCS: Modulation and
Coding Scheme. If we have a modulation which would give 1Mbps but a channel coding
with rate of 0.5, the person in the end will only get half of the information.
Normally we have:

The following shows what a real system does, with reference to LTE:

Chapter_4_EE_381S_Spg_2019,
p.25Chapter_4_EE_381S_Spg_2019, p.25
the dialogue between station and device is "go to MCS n". Those are thus modulating and
coding schemes.
When we use channel coding, the probability of error depending on the SNR with the LDPC
coding is fixed and then abruptly goes down; there's a sort of iterative technique and
when we have an iterative technique and we're in a bad situation, we're always there.
The first turbo engines in the car had the same problem: the turbo was kicking in all
of a sudden, as it had a multiplicative effect.
Something turbo starts later and when it does god down by a lot, reaching 1/2:

Rate of 1/2
(I think!)
As we lower SNR, we have less transmission but higher capability of correcting the
error: we're lowering the raw bit rate, this because our error probability is much higher
(and thus we'll need to focus on giving a robust message, rather than focusing on the
amount of transmitted bits).
We have poor modulation and we patch that with a good channel coding

We can draw a line like the one with QPSK, forgetting about the coding:

This shows what happens as we switch between MCS.
Depending on the SNR, we're limiting the capacity (or rather, we're choosing how to
treat the signal in order to achieve a good spectral efficiency). Of course we change
modulation and coding in order to stay as close as possible to the capacity curve.
We need to notice there always is a gap.
We see that for the graphs above we had a pretty high amount of bits in a block,
.

N = 6480

This is called bit adaptation: this was used when EDGE was introduced; this is like 2.5G.
The idea was to use the same modulator and change the coding scheme. With Edge we
see we can't open a web page. At the time though it was a big achievement. We can
read emails pretty well using EDGE.
We just adapt modulation and coding to the condition of the link. We of course need the
cooperation of the mobile, as it should report the bitrate it sees and what the SNR is.

18 to 08.4_pp_209_294_Single-user_SISO, page 19; we pretty much skipped it.

The second observation is extremely cryptic, thus I'll just copy it from the book:
08.4_pp_209_294_Single-user_SISO, page 24
As we know, it is necessary that N → ∞ for the mutual information to have the
operational interpretation of a spectral efficiency that can be achieved reliably. This
is compatible with a finite K as long as the codewords stretch over multiple
subblocks/OFDM symbols, and the operational significance is then acquired as the
number of such subblocks/OFDM symbols grows large. Both vector coding and OFDM
have an overhead of L for each K codeword symbols, with such overhead taking the
form of an idle guard interval in vector coding and of a cyclic prefix in OFDM. These
overheads only vanish for
achieved.

K → ∞

and thus only then can the capacity be truly

​My interpretation of it
What I think they were trying to say is that the mutual information coincides with
spectral efficiency only when we consider many many sub-blocks of length K . We
then have that the guard interval has no penalty if K → ∞.

Third observation is that for large N , the gap between vector coding (better) and OFDM
shrinks because Toeplitz matrices are asymptotically diagonalized by DFT matrices.
This is really similar to OFDM and we see that we have eigenvalues in here as well. If we
think about how professor showed us OFDM, he specifically highlighted that exponentials
are eigenfunctions with eigenvalues which are the Fourier transform at certain subcarrier
frequencies.
This should

not

be

difficult

to

remember,

as

we

have

a

structure

of

eigenfunctions/eigenvalues.
If we think of the OFDM matrices, those are discrete Fourier transform matrices, which
are unitary as well.

We're

really

accumulating

evidence

that

although

we

described

OFDM

as

signal

processing, it has a mathematical structure behind it. If we select as bases the
matrices of DFT, we don't even need to apply SVD as we'd already have U , U : we're
∗

rotating in a way that the system gets simpler to us.
This is not found in many books.
We see that the structure is exactly the same. We can then ask what the eigenvalues
are: they are the different subcarriers.
Then we'll have a certain power allocation for each subcarrier. When a certain subcarrier
has a very low value, it does not make sense to send power there; it makes sense to
send it where the power is better.
In-class randomness
A semi-definite positive matrix has eigenvalues which are either positive or zero;
here since we need to have something positive, we have the modulus squared.
Behind, there's a mathematical structure which is somewhat the same.
Behind there's a Toeplitz matrix, in which a row is the cyclic rotation of the previous
one: we use such matrices in order to represent convolutions. What happened is that
Toepliz matrices are getting asymptotically diagonalized by the DFT matrix. The idea
is that if we use the DFT as a basis, we can transform a convolution in a set of
parallel channels.
What we said about exponentials being eigenfunctions of the filters is of help now,
even if we're in a different context.
Of course we're in the infinite block regime. We can look at the problem from
different points of view, but we still get eigenvalues/vectors/functions.
If we studied functions, spaces, ..., we'd see that these kind of things are
becoming very clear, as we'd see the structure behind this.

which is a convex optimization problem with an equality constraint which can thus be
solved through the method of Lagrange multipliers. We know that the data rate is
normalized by the length of the block, but 1/K is a constant, thus we can maximize the
remaining part with respect to power constraints: we're trying to understand how to

distribute power across the various eigenvalues. There's a big part of mathematics
which is used in optimization theory: it is convex optimization.
We should now demonstrate that this maximization problem is convex (we won't tho
lol); this is because if we want to optimize something and we have a concave function,
maximization can be translated to minimization and we'll have a point at which we'll
have a minimum. There are some techniques doing so, like gradient descent. If it is
concave/convex, it's easy to solve numerically! Specifically, here we can use Lagrange
multipliers. We form the Lagrangian, which is given by what we want to maximize plus
the constraints:

The [⋅] is needed in order to say that we don't allocate power if the content of

08.4_pp_209_294_Single-user_SISO, page 26
In the time-invariant channels considered in this section, the value of K should in
principle be as large as can be afforded so as to minimize the relative overhead
.
L

K+L

However, once mobility is taken into account this needs to be restrained to ensure

that each OFDM symbol is short enough relative to the coherence time. The practice
is thus to set K to the smallest possible value that renders the overhead “small.”
Note, by identifying their respective reciprocals, how the condition that the delay
spread be “small” relative to the OFDM symbol period is mapped, in the frequency
domain, onto the requirement that the subcarrier spacing be “small” relative to the
coherence bandwidth. While this may agree with the intuition that each OFDM
subcarrier be narrow enough to experience a (roughly) frequency-flat channel
response, such frequency flatness over each subcarrier is not required for
orthogonality; it suffices that K > L for the subcarriers to be orthogonal at the
discrete points h[0], … , h[K − 1] and hence for the parallel subchannels to be
noninteracting. Frequency flatness over each subcarrier is merely the frequencydomain interpretation of the condition that ensures low overhead. At the same time,
as mentioned, mobility requires the OFDM subcarriers to be wide enough to withstand
the necessary Doppler spreads. It is to be celebrated that, as argued in Section
3.4.5, in underspread channels there is a broad enough gap between the Doppler
spread and the coherence bandwidth for subcarriers to be wide relative to the former
while narrow relative to the latter. Low overhead and robustness in the face of
mobility are therefore compatible.
An intuitive representation is the following:

Where the channel is better, the dashed part is lower, thus we put more power.
The idea is that we start from the best carrier, we put there power until we reach the
SNR of another carrier and then start pouring at that carrier as well.
​How do we obtain waterfilling from (4.104)?
The basic idea is that we can change η; we need to find a value of η which respects
the bound (4.105): such a value will imply a certain distribution of power which is
greater when the SNR is big, thus when the channel is good. In order to avoid
allocating negative power, we used [⋅] .
Then the procedure is the following:
+

1. Try solving (4.105) without the [⋅]
which follow by finding η.

+

constraint, so to get temporary values of P[k],

2. If any value of P[k] is negative, then that is definitively set to 0.
3. If in the previous point any value was set to 0, repeat from the previous point.
This can also be seen in example 4.16.

This is the best way to distribute power!!
We start from the best channel. If we had only 2 subcarriers, one of which has SNR of
0, we won't give any power to the subcarrier which is wasting our power; we put all
the power to the subcarrier which actually uses it for good.
The maximized information will amount to

We see that the overall SNR for a channel is modified by the gain on a subcarrier and the
power we're allocating. That is the same expression as a Gaussian channel.
The book then tells us how to compute the actual capacity: we need to remember that
when doing waterfilling we have a certain amount of subcarriers. Point is that in reality
we have something continuous, which is the reason why we want to push K → ∞:

We can get close to the continuous FT by increasing the amount of carriers towards
infinity.
Citing this directly from the book:

and we have that the edge effect caused by this augmentation will vanish as K grows.

channel behave like an OFDM transformation, yielding that for large K , vector coding will
converge to OFDM, meaning that we can consider OFDM as the asymptote.
If we then consider OFDM when K → ∞, we have that the frequency resolution becomes
increasingly finer (the subcarriers shrink to occupy less and less bandwidth) and the
DFTs become discrete-time Fourier transforms on the interval [−1/2, 1/2]; we then have
that (4.100) leads to capacity
